[36] DEDICATION
[37] This study is dedicated to all drivers who continue to face the risks and challenges brought by drowsiness while driving. May this work serve as a meaningful step toward promoting safer driving practices through technology, real-time monitoring, and alert systems that can help address drowsiness-related driving risks.
[38] This study is also dedicated to the researchers’ parents for their constant encouragement, support, and guidance, and to God for the wisdom, strength, and perseverance that guided every step of this endeavor.

[41] Additionally, this study is dedicated to future researchers and innovators who will continue to explore technological solutions for road-safety support and driver monitoring.
[42] This dedication extends to all individuals and organizations working to promote safer roads and protect lives through research, innovation, and responsible use of technology. To everyone who believes in the power of technology to create meaningful change and improve safety, this study is for you.

[501] CHAPTER I
[502] INTRODUCTION
[503] Safe driving depends not only on road conditions, traffic regulations, and vehicle performance but also on the driver’s ability to remain alert while operating a vehicle. Driver drowsiness is a serious road-safety concern because reduced alertness can impair attention, reaction time, judgment, and decision-making while driving. In the Philippine setting, the Land Transportation Office, as reported by the Philippine Information Agency, emphasized that drivers should pull over and rest when they feel tired. Similarly, Fonseca and Ferreira identified driver drowsiness as a significant road-safety issue associated with impaired attentional control, slower reaction time, reduced decision-making ability, and an increased risk of crashes and severe injuries (Philippine Information Agency, 2025; Fonseca & Ferreira, 2025). When drivers continue operating a vehicle despite visible signs of drowsiness, their ability to maintain the sustained attention required for safe driving may become compromised.
[504] In the Philippines, road traffic crashes remain a major public-safety issue. The World Health Organization Philippines and the Department of Transportation reported that road traffic deaths in the country increased by 39%, from 7,938 deaths in 2011 to 11,096 deaths in 2021. The same source reported that road traffic injuries are the leading cause of death among Filipinos aged 15 to 29 years old and cost approximately 2.6% of the country’s gross domestic product. In response to this concern, the Philippine Road Safety Action Plan 2023–2028 was launched as the country’s blueprint for road-safety initiatives, with the goal of reducing road traffic deaths by 35% by 2028 (World Health Organization Philippines & Department of Transportation, 2023). The Philippine Statistics Authority also reported that deaths due to land transport accidents reached 13,125 in 2023, accounting for 1.9% of all deaths in the country. Meanwhile, the Asian Transport Observatory reported that road crashes and serious injuries cost the Philippines approximately USD 11 billion in 2021, equivalent to about 3% of the country’s gross domestic product (Philippine Statistics Authority, 2025; Asian Transport Observatory, 2025). Although Philippine statistics do not separately identify the number of crashes associated with driver drowsiness, Reyes (2025) reported that international estimates attribute up to 30% of road accidents to driver fatigue. The same report explained that sleepiness associated with drowsy driving slows reaction time, decreases awareness, impairs judgment, and increases the risk of crashes. These conditions demonstrate the need for preventive and technology-supported road-safety systems that can assist drivers before dangerous situations become more serious.
[505] Human behavior and driver condition are also important considerations in road safety. Segun et al. (2024) identified human behavior, public awareness, infrastructure, technological interventions, and enforcement as factors associated with road-crash incidents in Metro Manila. Road safety therefore involves not only road and vehicle conditions but also the driver’s physical and mental condition. Because drowsiness may reduce alertness and responsiveness, technologies that monitor visible signs of drowsiness may help drivers recognize possible declines in attentiveness while driving.
[506] Emerging technologies in intelligent transportation provide new methods for detecting possible driver drowsiness before it develops into a more serious safety risk. Computer vision and artificial intelligence have been widely explored in driver-drowsiness detection systems because they can analyze visible indicators such as eye closure, yawning, mouth movement, and changes in head posture. Albadawi et al. (2022) explained that advances in computing technology and artificial intelligence have contributed to the development of more capable driver-drowsiness detection systems. However, the practical implementation of these systems may still be affected by lighting conditions, device capability, detection reliability, and system accessibility. These limitations highlight the need for a driver-monitoring system designed to operate under practical in-vehicle conditions.
[507] The background of this study is grounded in the integration of computer vision, artificial intelligence, the Internet of Things, and mobile-based driver monitoring. Camera-based systems can observe selected visible signs of drowsiness without requiring wearable sensors or direct vehicle integration. Lighting conditions remain an important consideration because camera-based facial measurements depend on usable image quality. DriveAlert uses an infrared-capable camera, and its performance under different available-light conditions will be evaluated using the final hardware configuration. Local processing is also important because warning systems should respond promptly even when internet access or mobile data is unavailable. Edge and localized processing can reduce latency and support privacy-sensitive Internet of Things applications, while ESP32 documentation supports Wi-Fi capability and local communication between the IoT device and the smartphone (Andriulo et al., 2024; Espressif Systems, 2025; Espressif Systems, 2026). When combined with Android-based processing and local offline Wi-Fi communication, these technologies can support a more accessible driver-drowsiness monitoring system.
[508] This study proposes DriveAlert, an Internet of Things-based driver drowsiness monitoring and alert system that uses computer vision and artificial intelligence. The system is designed to monitor selected visible signs of drowsiness, specifically prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding. When a selected behavior satisfies its established duration, repetition, or temporal-confirmation condition, DriveAlert activates a warning alert. Threshold-based alerting is appropriate because driver-drowsiness detection systems should not rely on a single uncertain video frame and should instead evaluate whether a visible behavior is sustained or repeated before activating a warning (Albadawi et al., 2023; Essel et al., 2025). DriveAlert also stores distinct confirmed warning events for Driver review and applies staged intervention when qualifying warning activity recurs or persists. Warning Stage 1 provides the initial selected warning sound, and Warning Stage 2 retains that sound as an intermediate warning state. Warning Stage 3 combines the selected sound with a prerecorded spoken rest advisory and introduces Trusted Contact synchronization eligibility. These Warning Stages represent levels of system intervention and do not represent medical or physiological drowsiness severity.
[509] Rationale of the Study
[510] Driver drowsiness may not always be recognized immediately while a person is operating a vehicle. Although drivers may stop and rest once they become aware that they are sleepy, observable changes associated with reduced alertness may appear before they respond to their condition. Rajkumarsingh and Totah (2021) explained that drowsiness can affect judgment and decision-making, while Ayas et al. (2024) found that driver-state monitoring systems commonly combine detection with warning interventions. This creates a need for a supplementary system that can monitor selected visible signs of drowsiness and provide a timely warning without replacing the driver’s responsibility to stop and rest.
[511] Visible changes involving the eyes, mouth, and head are commonly examined in driver-drowsiness monitoring because they can be observed without attaching physiological sensors to the driver. Rajkumarsingh and Totah (2021) considered blinking duration, yawning frequency, and head position, while Albadawi et al. (2023) examined eye condition, mouth opening, and head pose as visual features associated with drowsiness. These findings are relevant to the selected indicators monitored by DriveAlert: prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding. Monitoring these behaviors provides a non-contact method of identifying observable changes that may be associated with reduced alertness.
[512] However, ordinary driver behavior may resemble a visible sign of drowsiness. Brief eye closure may represent normal blinking, mouth opening may occur while speaking, and downward head movement may result from briefly looking at another area inside the vehicle. Responding to every isolated movement may therefore produce unnecessary warnings. Albadawi et al. (2023) evaluated visual indicators across a temporal window, while Quiles-Cucarella et al. (2024) and Essel et al. (2025) applied time-based or consecutive-frame conditions before activating warnings. These approaches demonstrate the importance of confirming that a behavior is sustained or repeated rather than relying on a single uncertain observation.
[513] Warning reliability is also important because detection has limited value when the resulting alert is unclear, easily ignored, or repeatedly activated by incorrect detections. Ayas et al. (2024) found that driver-drowsiness mitigation systems commonly use auditory warnings and may apply different levels of intervention. Weibull et al. (2024) also showed that false alarms can influence how drivers respond to later warnings. A drowsiness-warning system must therefore balance responsiveness and consistency so that the driver is alerted when the established detection conditions are satisfied without producing excessive interruptions during ordinary movements.
[514] DriveAlert applies three Warning Stages to manage recurrent or persistent qualifying warning activity. The first distinct confirmed warning event occurring while no Warning Stage is active immediately activates Stage 1 and begins a 60-second evaluation period. If another distinct confirmed warning event occurs while Stage 1 is active, the system immediately progresses to Stage 2 and begins a new 60-second evaluation period. A subsequent distinct confirmed warning event during Stage 2 immediately activates Stage 3. When no new distinct event occurs, the system evaluates whether a qualifying behavior remains persistently active at the 60-second boundary. Persistent qualifying activity causes progression from Stage 1 to Stage 2 or from Stage 2 to Stage 3. If no qualifying behavior remains active, Stage 1 returns to the normal state, while Stage 2 de-escalates to Stage 1. Stage 3 is the maximum intervention level. Recurrent events during Stage 3 may reactivate its warning without restarting the current evaluation period. At the Stage 3 boundary, recurrent or persistent qualifying activity maintains Stage 3; otherwise, the system de-escalates to Stage 2. Stages 1 and 2 use the selected warning sound, while Stage 3 adds a spoken rest advisory and Trusted Contact synchronization eligibility. These stages describe system intervention rather than medical or physiological drowsiness severity.
[515] An immediate warning addresses a detected condition at a particular moment, but the warning alone provides little information after it has ended. Without an organized record, the driver may be unable to review when an alert occurred, which visible sign was detected, or whether alerts appeared repeatedly during a monitoring session. Hidayat and Ibnugraha (2025) developed a system described as a driver-fatigue monitoring system that displayed detection results through a web-based dashboard, while Kashyap et al. (2025) stored driver-status information, detection timestamps, and historical records for later review. These studies show that recorded events can remain useful beyond the immediate warning by providing structured information for later review.
[516] Stored drowsiness-alert events may also be summarized to make the recorded information easier to interpret. Retained alerts may be organized into alert counts, detected-sign summaries, the highest Warning Stage associated with their stored warning events, alert trends, and Stage 3 synchronization status. Monitoring-session summaries separately identify the highest intervention stage reached during each session. These summaries allow users to review patterns in the available records without examining every alert individually. Their purpose is descriptive and should not be interpreted as medical analysis, accident prediction, confirmation of drowsiness, or proof that a driver is safe or unsafe to continue driving.
[517] Drowsiness-alert records may also support secondary awareness through an approved Trusted Contact. DriveAlert stores all distinct confirmed warning events for Driver review but limits external synchronization according to its Warning Stage 3 rule. When DriveAlert first enters Warning Stage 3, one Stage 3 transition record becomes eligible for Trusted Contact synchronization. Individual confirmed warning events occurring during the active Stage 3 period remain in the Driver’s alert history and do not each independently create synchronization records. When a 60-second Stage 3 evaluation period ends, DriveAlert creates one synchronization-eligible persistence update if at least one additional distinct confirmed warning event occurred during that period or a qualifying behavior remains persistently active at its boundary. Either condition maintains Stage 3 and begins another 60-second evaluation period. If neither condition is present, the system de-escalates to Stage 2 without creating a persistence update for the completed period.
[518] The reviewed approaches provide visible-sign monitoring, warning delivery, event storage, dashboard viewing, physical alarm output, offline operation, and authorized record access in different combinations. Some systems concentrate mainly on detecting a visible sign and producing an immediate warning, while others emphasize historical records, dashboards, or remote monitoring. The need addressed by this study is therefore not the absence of driver-drowsiness technology but the opportunity to combine selected functions that support the driver during monitoring and preserve relevant information after a warning has been issued.
[519] DriveAlert addresses these connected needs by monitoring prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding and providing an audio warning when a selected behavior satisfies its established detection condition. It also stores distinct confirmed alert episodes locally, presents descriptive summaries of retained records for Driver review, and synchronizes only eligible Stage 3 transition records and Stage 3 persistence updates for access by an approved Trusted Contact when internet connectivity becomes available. Through this integration, DriveAlert connects visible-sign monitoring, warning delivery, event recording, record review, and authorized information sharing within one driver-focused system. It is intended as a supplementary driver-support tool and does not medically or physiologically confirm drowsiness, determine whether a driver is fit to continue driving, replace adequate rest, or control the vehicle.
[520] 
[521] 
[522] Objectives of the Study
[523] This study aims to develop DriveAlert, an Internet of Things-based system designed to assist drivers by monitoring selected visible signs of drowsiness, providing warning alerts, storing drowsiness-alert records, and synchronizing these records with a connected Trusted Contact when internet connectivity becomes available.
[524] Specifically, this study pursues the following objectives: 
[525] To gather data on the occurrence of drowsiness and observable signs associated with it among drivers while driving.
[526] To design and implement a computer-vision algorithm capable of detecting prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding using driver-specific detection thresholds and temporal-confirmation conditions for triggering warning alerts.
[527] To design and implement a local Wi-Fi communication architecture between the IoT device and the driver’s smartphone that enables real-time drowsiness-sign monitoring without requiring internet or mobile data connectivity.
[528] To implement an alarm system in which the IoT device receives wireless warning commands from the smartphone and produces predefined audio warning alerts through an onboard speaker.
[529] To develop a dashboard that records and displays drowsiness-alert events and synchronizes drowsiness-alert records with a connected Trusted Contact when internet connectivity becomes available.
[530] Scope and Limitations
[531] This study focuses on the development and pilot evaluation of DriveAlert, an IoT-based driver drowsiness monitoring and alert system using computer vision. The system monitors prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding through an IoT device equipped with an infrared-capable camera and an onboard speaker. Dedicated infrared LEDs and external infrared illuminators are excluded from the final prototype.
[532] An Android mobile application processes the captured video locally using calibrated EAR and MAR thresholds, a fixed 15° downward head-pitch deviation relative to the Driver’s calibrated neutral position, and sign-specific temporal-confirmation conditions. After a selected visible sign satisfies its complete confirmation condition, DriveAlert creates a confirmed warning event, applies the appropriate Warning Stage, requests the corresponding warning output, and stores the event locally for Driver review. Separate Stage 3 transition records and qualifying Stage 3 persistence updates become eligible for synchronization with an approved Trusted Contact when internet connectivity becomes available.
[533] DriveAlert is intended for drivers of four-wheeled and larger vehicles. Motorcycles and other two-wheeled vehicles are excluded because of differences in driver position, camera placement, weather exposure, and operating conditions. The system will be evaluated only under controlled or simulated driving conditions and will not be tested for long-term use during actual road travel, heavy traffic, severe weather, uneven roads, or other unpredictable conditions.
[534] DriveAlert is limited to monitoring three selected visible signs associated with drowsiness: prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding. The system does not medically or physiologically confirm that a driver is drowsy. These behaviors may occur for reasons unrelated to drowsiness, while a drowsy driver may not always display them clearly. The system may therefore generate false alerts or miss relevant events. It does not detect sleep disorders, stress, illness, medication effects, alcohol influence, or other causes of reduced alertness, and it does not use physiological or wearable sensors.
[535] Detection performance may be affected by camera placement, Driver position, head orientation, facial obstruction, eyeglasses, sunglasses, masks, glare, lighting conditions, sudden movement, reflections, and image quality. Although dedicated infrared illumination was included in the initial design, it was excluded from the final prototype due to limitations encountered during hardware development. The available infrared LED configuration did not provide sufficient facial illumination at the intended camera-to-Driver distance. Attempts to improve illumination by increasing the number of infrared LEDs also resulted in unstable operation of the breadboard power supply setup, including intermittent power loss and interruptions affecting connected components. These issues prevented the expanded LED configuration from operating reliably and limited further improvements to infrared illumination using the available hardware. These limitations apply specifically to the configuration tested in this study and do not establish that infrared illumination is unsuitable for driver monitoring in general. The final prototype therefore depends on available light, and its infrared-capable camera does not guarantee reliable assessment under extremely low-light or no-light conditions. DriveAlert pauses drowsiness-sign assessment when facial lighting is insufficient, even if a face remains detected. Assessment also pauses when reliable facial tracking is unavailable or fewer than two monitored signs remain available. Frames rejected because of insufficient lighting do not advance calibration’s required valid measurement time. Assessment resumes when the applicable visibility, lighting-quality, and observation-freshness requirements are restored. These assessment pauses do not by themselves end the monitoring session. DriveAlert also provides operational guidance that is separate from drowsiness-stage warnings. During eligible active monitoring, sustained inactivity may produce a “No movement detected” status after ten seconds and a spoken movement advisory after twenty-five seconds. These timings are provisional engineering settings. The advisory does not create a drowsiness-alert record, advance the Warning Stage, verify Driver identity, or establish that the camera input is from a live person.
[536] DriveAlert uses guided facial-measurement calibration and spatial Driver-area selection rather than facial recognition or automatic Driver identification. Calibration records neutral facial measurements, closed-eye and open-mouth references, and the direction of downward head rotation. The face-processing pipeline may detect multiple face candidates, but drowsiness-sign assessment uses at most one selected Driver face. Selection depends on the configured Driver area and spatial continuity. Ambiguous, competing, or unreliable detections pause assessment. These safeguards do not verify identity or guarantee the exclusion of passengers in every scene. Camera movement, incorrect positioning, other visible faces, or changes in the Driver’s position may affect tracking and detection. When reliable facial tracking is unavailable, assessment pauses and the Driver is prompted to restore the camera view.
[537] The system does not automatically identify individual drivers. In shared vehicles, each driver must use the correct DriveAlert account, connect to the IoT device, and verify camera alignment before monitoring begins. Otherwise, drowsiness-alert records may be stored under the wrong account. Facial recognition and automatic driver identification are outside the scope of the study.
[538] System performance may depend on the Android smartphone’s processing power, memory, battery condition, and temperature. Devices with limited capability may experience slower video processing, delayed warnings, or reduced application stability. The pilot evaluation will use selected compatible Android devices; therefore, the results may not represent all Android devices and hardware configurations.
[539] All distinct confirmed alert episodes are initially stored locally in the Driver’s alert history, subject to user deletion and configured retention. Descriptive alert summaries reflect retained records. Removing a shared record from a Trusted Contact’s personal history view does not delete the source Driver’s cloud record. DriveAlert uses three Warning Stages with 60-second evaluation periods. A new distinct confirmed event immediately advances Stage 1 to Stage 2 or Stage 2 to Stage 3 and begins a new evaluation period. When no new distinct event occurs, persistent qualifying behavior at the evaluation boundary may cause the same progression. The absence of qualifying activity permits incremental de-escalation and eventual return to the normal state. Stages 1 and 2 use the Driver’s selected warning sound, while Stage 3 adds a prerecorded spoken rest advisory and Trusted Contact synchronization eligibility. Each progression from Stage 2 to Stage 3 creates one transition record, including re-entry into Stage 3 after de-escalation. Each completed Stage 3 period creates at most one persistence update when an additional distinct event occurred during the period or a qualifying behavior remains persistently active at its boundary. Individual warning events remain in the Driver’s local history and do not independently create synchronization records. Sharing requires an approved Trusted Contact relationship and internet connectivity. Only eligible records from periods beginning on or after connection approval may be shared, and access depends on the relationship remaining approved. This feature supports secondary awareness and does not operate as an immediate emergency-notification service. Warning Stages do not represent medical or physiological drowsiness severity.
[540] The study does not measure DriveAlert’s effect on accident reduction, injury prevention, driver behavior change, or long-term driving habits. It does not cover commercial deployment, vehicle-manufacturer integration, vehicle-control systems, traffic enforcement, or automotive safety certification. DriveAlert cannot apply the brakes, steer the vehicle, reduce its speed, stop the engine, or determine whether the driver is fit to continue driving.
[541] Local monitoring can operate without internet after the required account and device setup. Starting a monitoring session requires the appropriate Driver account, an active calibration, a configured and connected camera, and confirmed camera alignment. Account registration, cloud relationship management, remote record review, and external synchronization require internet connectivity.
[542] The Android Driver interface supports local monitoring, alert-history review, and descriptive summaries. Trusted Contact interfaces provide access to eligible shared Stage 3 records rather than the Driver’s complete local alert history or camera stream. Record synchronization and notification delivery are separate processes; successful synchronization does not establish that a Trusted Contact has received or viewed a notification.
[543] DriveAlert is not intended to replace adequate rest, responsible driving practices, professional medical evaluation, or certified vehicle-safety systems. The study is limited to evaluating whether drowsiness-sign monitoring, warning activation, drowsiness-alert record management, dashboard functions, and Trusted Contact synchronization can operate together under controlled or simulated testing conditions.
[544] Significance of the Study
[545] The study brings benefits to various stakeholders involved in or affected by the development and implementation of DriveAlert.
[546] Drivers. Drivers are the primary beneficiaries of DriveAlert. They may benefit from real-time monitoring of selected visible signs of drowsiness, predefined audio warning alerts with adjustable volume and an enforced minimum level, and a dashboard that records and summarizes drowsiness-alert events. Through the dashboard, Drivers can review alert counts, detected-sign summaries, the highest Warning Stage associated with their stored warning events, alert trends, and Stage 3 synchronization status. Session summaries separately identify the highest intervention stage reached during an individual monitoring session.
[547] Trusted Contacts. Trusted Contacts may benefit from accessing synchronization-eligible Stage 3 transition records and Stage 3 persistence updates when internet connectivity becomes available. These records provide secondary awareness when qualifying warning activity reaches or continues at DriveAlert’s maximum intervention stage. The feature supports synchronized record review and does not function as live Driver monitoring or an emergency-response service.
[548] Passengers. Passengers may indirectly benefit from DriveAlert because the system provides a warning when a selected visible sign of drowsiness satisfies its established detection condition. The warning may prompt the driver to reassess their condition and take appropriate action, such as stopping and resting.
[549] Researchers. The researchers will gain practical experience in Internet of Things development, mobile application design, computer vision-based drowsiness-sign detection, local wireless communication, alert-system implementation, and system evaluation. The study will also strengthen their technical, analytical, problem-solving, documentation, and research skills.
[550] Future Researchers. Future researchers may use this study as a reference for related projects involving driver drowsiness monitoring, computer vision, IoT-based safety systems, mobile processing, offline-capable warning systems, alert-record management, and driver-assistance technologies.
[551] Flow of the Study
[552] The procedure involves identifying prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding as selected visible signs associated with drowsiness, processing the observed behaviors through the proposed system, and generating alerts and records when the established alert conditions are satisfied.

[596] Figure 1: Flow of the Study
[597] Figure 1 shows the flow of the “DriveAlert” study. The process begins with gathering and collecting data on observable signs of driver drowsiness, such as prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding.
[598] The next phase involves determining an algorithm that can monitor live video and detect selected visible signs of driver drowsiness, specifically prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding. This phase also includes applying Driver-specific detection thresholds together with sign-specific temporal-confirmation conditions to determine when a confirmed warning event is created.
[599] Following this, a direct and offline wireless connection will be designed between the IoT device and the smartphone of the driver. This connection allows the video to be processed instantly through the mobile application without depending on internet or mobile data.
[600] To complete the alerting process, an alarm system will be implemented where the IoT device receives wireless signals from the smartphone and triggers an onboard speaker. The onboard speaker will play predefined audio warning alerts using an adjustable warning volume that cannot be muted or reduced below the enforced minimum level during active monitoring.
[601] The study also includes the implementation of a mobile dashboard that allows the driver to review drowsiness alert history and basic drowsiness insights. The dashboard displays today’s drowsiness alerts, weekly alert count, synchronization status, most frequently detected sign, highest Warning Stage reached, alert trend, and synchronization summary. A background synchronization system will also be developed to automatically synchronize eligible Warning Stage 3 transition records and Stage 3 persistence updates with an approved Trusted Contact when internet connectivity becomes available.
[602] The study follows the Agile Methodology, progressing through iterative stages of planning, requirement analysis, system design, development, testing, deployment, and maintenance. This flexible approach supports continuous improvement and refinement based on feedback from users and testing environments.
[603] Ultimately, the output of this study is “DriveAlert,” an Internet of Things safety system designed to assist drivers through drowsiness-sign detection, warning alerts, drowsiness alert history recording, basic drowsiness insights, and Trusted Contact synchronization.
[604] Definition of Terms
[605] This section contains the specific meaning of the following terms concerning the context used to clarify the study.
[606] Alert Trend. It refers to the basic insight showing whether the number of recorded drowsiness alerts is higher, lower, or unchanged compared with the previous period. 
[607] Artificial Intelligence. It refers to the system capability used by DriveAlert to assist in analyzing driver video and identifying possible drowsiness-related signs.
[608] Computer Vision. It refers to the technology used by DriveAlert to process live video and examine visible movements of the driver’s eyes, mouth, face, and head.
[609] Driver. It refers to the primary user of DriveAlert who operates a four-wheeled or larger vehicle and uses the IoT device and mobile application to start monitoring sessions, receive drowsiness warnings, review drowsiness-alert records and summaries, and manage Trusted Contact access.
[610] Drowsiness Alert. It refers to a confirmed warning event generated after one or more selected visible signs satisfy DriveAlert’s complete Driver-specific measurement and sign-specific temporal-confirmation conditions
[611] False Alert. It refers to a warning generated when ordinary or non-target driver behavior incorrectly satisfies the system’s alert condition. 
[612] Fleet Manager. It refers to a person responsible for supervising a group of organizational or commercial vehicles and their drivers. In the current DriveAlert design, Fleet Manager is identified only as a potential customer segment and not as a separate system actor.
[613] Highest Warning Stage. It refers to the highest Warning Stage within the context being summarized. A monitoring-session summary reports the highest intervention stage reached during that session, while an alert-history summary reports the highest stage associated with the stored warning events included in that summary. Neither value represents the Driver’s medical or physiological level of drowsiness.
[614] Internet of Things. It refers to the connected hardware and software components of DriveAlert that allow the IoT device and mobile application to exchange video data, connection information, and warning commands.
[615] IoT Device. It refers to the DriveAlert hardware unit containing the infrared-capable camera, wireless communication components, and onboard speaker used during driver monitoring.
[616] Local Offline Wi-Fi Connection. It refers to the direct local network between the DriveAlert IoT device and the driver’s Android smartphone that does not require internet access or mobile data.
[617] Local Processing. It refers to the processing of the transmitted driver video through the Android smartphone instead of sending the video to an internet-based cloud service for drowsiness-sign detection.
[618] Missed Detection. It refers to a situation in which the system fails to identify a selected drowsiness-related sign that is visibly demonstrated by the driver.
[619] Monitoring Session. It refers to the period beginning when the driver starts drowsiness monitoring and ending when monitoring is stopped. Drowsiness alerts detected during this period are associated with the same session.
[620] Onboard Speaker. It refers to the physical speaker installed in the DriveAlert IoT device that produces the selected audio warning after receiving an alert command from the smartphone.
[621] Prolonged Eye Closure. It refers to the driver’s eyes remaining closed long enough to satisfy the established duration condition, distinguishing it from an ordinary brief blink.
[622] Temporal Confirmation. It refers to the requirement that an observed behavior continue or repeat over a defined period or across consecutive video frames before the system treats the behavior as meeting its established alert threshold.
[623] Threshold. It refers to the predefined duration, repetition, movement, or other condition that a detected drowsiness-related sign must satisfy before DriveAlert generates a warning.
[624] Trusted Contact. It refers to a user connected to a Driver through an approved relationship who may review eligible shared Stage 3 transition records and Stage 3 persistence updates. The role does not provide access to the Driver’s complete local alert history or full Driver-facing insights. Access depends on the relationship remaining approved. A Trusted Contact does not control monitoring or function as an emergency-response service.
[625] Trusted Contact Synchronization. It refers to the automatic upload of eligible Stage 3 transition records and Stage 3 persistence updates for access by an approved Trusted Contact when internet connectivity becomes available. Sharing is limited to eligible records from periods beginning on or after connection approval and does not release the Driver’s earlier complete alert history. Removing the approved relationship revokes the Trusted Contact’s access.
[626] Warning Stage Progression. It refers to the process through which DriveAlert changes its active intervention stage. A new distinct confirmed warning event immediately advances Stage 1 to Stage 2 or Stage 2 to Stage 3 and begins a new 60-second evaluation period. Persistent qualifying behavior may also cause escalation at an evaluation boundary. At Stage 3, recurrent or persistent qualifying activity maintains the maximum stage, while the absence of qualifying activity permits incremental de-escalation. Stage progression does not represent increasing medical or physiological drowsiness severity.
[627] Warning Trigger. It refers to the system action that activates or reissues the warning associated with the applicable Warning Stage. A warning may be triggered by a new distinct confirmed warning event or by persistent qualifying behavior evaluated at a warning-stage boundary. Recurrent qualifying events during Stage 3 may also reactivate its warning without creating an additional stage.
[628] Warning Stage. It refers to the current state of DriveAlert’s warning intervention. Stage 1 provides the initial selected warning sound. Stage 2 retains the same sound as an intermediate warning state. Stage 3 represents the maximum intervention level, combines the selected sound with a prerecorded spoken rest advisory, and introduces Trusted Contact synchronization eligibility. Each stage uses a 60-second evaluation period, although new distinct confirmed events may cause earlier progression. Warning Stages do not represent medical or physiological levels of drowsiness.
