[659] CHAPTER II
[660] REVIEW OF RELATED LITERATURE AND STUDIES
[661] This chapter presents the related literature and studies gathered through the researchers’ careful review and analysis. It provides the theoretical and technical foundation for the development of DriveAlert: An IoT-Based Driver Drowsiness Monitoring and Alert System Using Computer Vision. The review discusses concepts and studies related to driver fatigue and drowsiness, vigilance, human-centered warning systems, computer vision, Internet of Things, mobile-based detection, offline communication, alert mechanisms, drowsiness alert history recording, and Trusted Contact synchronization. This chapter also establishes how the proposed system relates to existing approaches and how DriveAlert contributes to the development of driver drowsiness monitoring and alert systems.
[662] Theoretical Background
[663] This study is anchored primarily in Vigilance Theory, which explains how sustained attention and alertness may decline during prolonged monitoring tasks. Mackworth (1948) introduced the concept of vigilance decrement through research showing that a person’s ability to detect important signals may decrease over time. In driving, this theory is relevant because drivers must maintain attention for extended periods, particularly during nighttime travel, long-distance driving, and monotonous road conditions. McWilliams and Ward (2021) explained that low-demand and monotonous driving conditions may place drivers in a state of cognitive underload, contributing to vigilance decrement and reduced responsiveness to safety-critical events. For DriveAlert, Vigilance Theory provides a conceptual basis for understanding why maintaining alertness is important during prolonged or monotonous driving. The system itself is limited to monitoring prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding as selected visible signs associated with drowsiness.
[664] The study is further guided by Human Factors Engineering and Human-Centered Design principles. Human factors focus on designing systems according to human capabilities, limitations, behaviors, and possible errors. The Federal Highway Administration (n.d.) explained that human factors address the relationship between people and the systems they use, with consideration for human abilities and limitations. This is relevant to driver monitoring because warnings should be clear, timely, noticeable, and understandable without requiring complex interaction from the driver. Ayas et al. (2024) found that driver-drowsiness mitigation systems commonly use auditory warnings and may employ multistage interventions. These principles support DriveAlert’s use of predefined audio warnings, warning escalation, clear monitoring status, and an interface designed to minimize driver interaction during active monitoring while allowing drowsiness-alert records to be reviewed afterward.
[665] Together, Vigilance Theory and human-centered design principles provide the theoretical and design foundation of DriveAlert. Vigilance Theory explains the importance of maintaining sustained attention during prolonged or monotonous driving, while Human Factors Engineering and Human-Centered Design guide how the system should communicate warnings and present information according to human capabilities and limitations. These foundations support DriveAlert’s development as a driver-centered drowsiness monitoring and alert system that combines visible-sign monitoring, warning output, drowsiness-alert record review, basic drowsiness insights, and Trusted Contact synchronization.
[666] Related Literature
[667] Driver sleepiness, sleep deprivation, and prolonged working hours are associated with increased road-traffic risk. Saleem (2022) identified sleepiness and sleep deprivation as contributors to road traffic accidents, while the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (n.d.) documented crashes and fatalities involving drowsy drivers. In the Philippine setting, Lu (2022) reported that prolonged working hours among bus and truck drivers may contribute to fatigue and sleepiness, thereby increasing road-accident risk. Segun et al. (2024) further identified human behavior and technology-supported interventions among the factors relevant to road safety. Together, these studies establish driver drowsiness as a road-safety concern and support the development of systems that monitor Driver behavior and provide timely warnings.
[668] Driver fatigue and drowsiness are related but distinct concepts. Fatigue broadly refers to mental or physical exhaustion, whereas drowsiness describes a sleep-like reduction in alertness that may precede sleep onset. Although some literature uses fatigue as a broader term when discussing reduced alertness, DriveAlert is specifically limited to selected visible signs associated with drowsiness and does not claim to detect fatigue as a whole. Hassan et al. (2026) identified prolonged or slow eyelid closure, yawning, and head nodding as behavioral manifestations of drowsiness. Albadawi et al. (2022) and Jan et al. (2023) likewise identified eye closure, yawning, and head nodding among observable behaviors used in non-intrusive Driver-drowsiness detection. These findings support the selection of prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding as the three visible signs monitored by DriveAlert.
[669] Because these signs are externally observable, computer vision provides a non-invasive means of monitoring them without requiring physiological sensors. Albadawi et al. (2023) extracted Eye Aspect Ratio (EAR), Mouth Aspect Ratio (MAR), and head-pose features from facial landmarks using MediaPipe Face Mesh. Essel et al. (2025) similarly examined eye closure and mouth aperture, while Kim et al. (2023) used infrared video and facial landmarks to evaluate eye closure and head orientation. These studies demonstrate that facial landmarks can support a common visual method for examining eye-, mouth-, and head-related behavior. However, facial measurements may still be affected by lighting, camera placement, face orientation, eyeglasses, facial obstruction, and individual facial characteristics.
[670] Eye Aspect Ratio represents the degree of eye openness using the relationship among selected eye landmarks and generally decreases as the upper and lower eyelids move closer together. Mouth Aspect Ratio similarly represents the degree of mouth opening and generally increases as the mouth opens wider (Albadawi et al., 2023). Head-pose estimation uses facial reference points to estimate changes in head orientation, while pitch represents upward or downward head rotation and may therefore support head-nodding analysis (Albadawi et al., 2023; Kim et al., 2023). Since ordinary blinking, speaking, and brief downward glances can temporarily produce similar measurements, a single threshold-crossing frame is insufficient to confirm a selected visible sign.
[671] Previous studies provide numerical values that can serve as initial references for DriveAlert, but these values should not automatically be applied to every Driver. Albadawi et al. (2023) selected an EAR threshold of 0.20 after evaluating different candidate values and eye characteristics. Higher values increased the possibility of normally open but smaller eyes being classified as closed, while lower values reduced the number of detected closed-eye frames. The same study selected a MAR threshold of 0.50 after considering differences in mouth characteristics; values that were too high could miss wide-mouth events among subjects with smaller mouths, while values that were too low increased the likelihood of ordinary talking or laughing being classified as mouth opening. These findings support 0.20 for EAR and 0.50 for MAR as literature-supported reference values rather than universal operational thresholds.
[672] Individual differences support the use of Driver-specific thresholds because fixed facial measurements may not generalize across users with different facial characteristics and normal head orientations. DriveAlert therefore uses four guided calibration phases, each requiring five seconds of valid measurements. During the neutral phase, the Driver faces forward with the eyes open to establish baseline EAR, MAR, and neutral head pitch. During the eyes-closed phase, closed-eye EAR is recorded as a calibration reference, although the operational EAR threshold remains 75% of the neutral open-eye baseline following Ersoy et al. (2026). During the mouth-open phase, the Driver keeps the mouth as open as possible, and the operational MAR threshold is set at 50% of the average open-mouth MAR following the personalized initialization approach of Quiles-Cucarella et al. (2024), expressed as MARThreshold = OpenMouthMAR × 0.50. During the head-down phase, pitch measurements determine the direction of downward rotation relative to the neutral position. DriveAlert then applies a 15° downward deviation from the calibrated neutral head pitch as its operational head-pitch threshold. Xu et al. (2025) similarly used normal forward orientation as a reference, while Shang et al. (2023) reported drowsy head-pitch changes of approximately 12° to 20°, placing the selected 15° deviation within the reported range. These Driver-specific calibration values are stored and used during monitoring.
[673] For prolonged eye closure, DriveAlert will use 2.0 seconds as its initial operational confirmation duration. Dong et al. (2026) analyzed distributions of normal and fatigue-related continuous eye-closure events and reported that separation between them became stable at approximately two seconds. Their system therefore used sustained eye closure exceeding two seconds as a fatigue-related criterion. Albadawi et al. (2023) also reported that ordinary blinking occurs on a substantially shorter timescale. DriveAlert consequently adopts 2.0 seconds only to determine whether an observed eye closure qualifies as prolonged eye closure and does not interpret the duration as medical confirmation that the Driver is drowsy. Prolonged eye closure will therefore be confirmed when the Driver's EAR remains below the Driver-specific EAR threshold continuously for at least 2.0 seconds. If the EAR reaches or rises above the threshold before the required duration is completed, the temporal counter resets and no prolonged-eye-closure event is confirmed.
[674] Yawning requires a different duration because mouth opening associated with yawning is naturally a longer behavior. Dong et al. (2026) analyzed sustained mouth-opening duration and reported that separation between ordinary mouth movements and fatigue-related yawning became stable at approximately three seconds. Their system consequently used sustained mouth opening exceeding three seconds as its yawning criterion. Quiles-Cucarella et al. (2024) likewise used a personalized MAR threshold together with a three-second mouth-opening condition. DriveAlert will therefore confirm yawning when the Driver’s MAR remains above the Driver-specific MAR threshold continuously for at least 3.0 seconds. If the MAR reaches or falls below the threshold before the required duration is completed, the temporal counter resets and no yawning event is confirmed.
[675] Head nodding requires a different confirmation approach because downward head movement is more ambiguous than sustained eye closure or mouth opening. A Driver may naturally look toward the dashboard, vehicle controls, or another location below the normal forward-facing position without being drowsy. Kholerdi et al. (2016) addressed this ambiguity by evaluating the persistence of downward or away-from-road head position rather than treating every downward movement as an immediate warning condition.
[676] Kholerdi et al. (2016) considered a prolonged head position significant when the Driver’s head remained dropped or turned away during at least 80% of the preceding three seconds. The authors explained that even when such a prolonged position was not caused by drowsiness, maintaining the head away from the normal forward-facing position for a substantial portion of the observation period could still warrant warning.
[677] DriveAlert will therefore initially treat downward head rotation exceeding a fixed 15° deviation from the Driver’s calibrated neutral head position as a candidate head-nodding event rather than immediately confirming the behavior. Calibration establishes the Driver’s neutral head pitch and the direction of downward rotation; it does not determine a different angular deviation threshold for each Driver. The candidate will be confirmed when downward rotation exceeds this threshold in at least 80% of valid observations within a rolling three-second period. If this persistence condition is not satisfied, no head-nodding event is confirmed. The persistence condition is adapted from Kholerdi et al. (2016), while the fixed 15° deviation is a DriveAlert operational choice informed by the approximately 12° to 20° head-pitch changes reported by Shang et al. (2023).
[678] The three selected signs are therefore not given identical confirmation procedures because they present different sources of false detection. Sustained EAR measurements help distinguish prolonged eye closure from ordinary blinking, while sustained MAR measurements help distinguish yawning from shorter mouth movements. Head movement is more ambiguous because normal Driver behavior can also involve downward pitch changes, making temporal persistence appropriate. DriveAlert consequently evaluates each selected sign according to its own characteristics rather than requiring prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding to occur simultaneously.
[679] During uninterrupted valid observation, DriveAlert groups additional confirmed signs into the current alert episode while a previously active episode sign remains currently confirmed. These additional signs do not independently create another alert episode or advance the Warning Stage. A continuously confirmed sign does not repeatedly create distinct events merely because its measurement remains beyond its threshold. Its detector must be re-armed before the same sign can produce another confirmation. Tracking loss, stale observations, or assessment interruptions may break episode continuity; the system does not assume that a physical behavior remained continuously observable during an unavailable interval.
[680] The adopted detection thresholds and confirmation conditions will be evaluated during controlled testing using the actual DriveAlert IoT camera and Android processing environment. Testing will examine false alerts, missed detections, Driver-specific differences, landmark stability, camera placement, performance under different available-light conditions, and processing performance. The purpose of this testing is to verify or refine literature-supported initial conditions under DriveAlert's intended implementation environment rather than assuming that numerical values obtained from another system will transfer perfectly.
[681] Computer vision also supports local and non-invasive system implementation. In the Philippine setting, Macalisang et al. (2021) developed Drive-Awake using machine vision to examine eye closure associated with drowsy driving. Fonseca and Ferreira (2025) reviewed behavioral, physiological, and multimodal Driver-monitoring approaches and emphasized that system performance may vary according to datasets, camera conditions, and implementation environments. These considerations support evaluating DriveAlert under its actual deployment conditions.
[682] Local mobile processing can reduce dependence on cloud infrastructure by processing data close to its source. Andriulo et al. (2024) discussed edge processing for connected devices, while Martinez-Alpiste et al. (2022) demonstrated the relevance of optimized real-time computer-vision processing for resource-constrained platforms. Li et al. (2024) and Tan and Cao (2024) further indicated that mobile inference may be affected by hardware capability, processing workload, battery condition, and temperature. These findings support DriveAlert's Android-based local processing while recognizing that smartphone capability may influence detection responsiveness.
[683] Fourie and Myburgh (2022) demonstrated communication between ESP32-CAM devices and an Android smartphone through a local wireless network for real-time computer-vision functions. Their implementation transmitted camera frames through local Wi-Fi while the smartphone performed processing, demonstrating the technical feasibility of ESP32-based camera hardware communicating with Android without relying on cloud inference. Espressif Systems documentation likewise establishes ESP32 Wi-Fi capability, supporting DriveAlert's local IoT-to-mobile communication architecture.
[684] Vehicle-mounted driver-monitoring systems must also consider road-safety regulations concerning device placement and driver visibility. In the Philippines, Republic Act No. 10913, or the Anti-Distracted Driving Act, regulates distracting uses of mobile communication and electronic computing devices while driving. Section 5(a) further establishes that the placement of hands-free mobile communication devices must not interfere with the driver's line of sight (Republic Act No. 10913, 2016). The Land Transportation Office (2021), through its Filipino Driver's Manual, Volume 2, illustrates safe device-placement areas and recommends positioning dashboard cameras behind the rearview mirror. Additionally, Joint Administrative Order No. 2014-01 addresses defective, improper, or unauthorized vehicle accessories and devices that are manifestly prejudicial to road safety (Department of Transportation and Communications et al., 2014). These regulatory considerations provide a basis for DriveAlert's physical design and proposed installation approach. Accordingly, the IoT enclosure and mounting arrangement should be designed to minimize obstruction of the driver's forward visibility, avoid interference with vehicle operation, and maintain a suitable camera view of the Driver. The applicability of these considerations to the final prototype will be assessed during controlled installation testing.
[685] Low-light monitoring is relevant because drowsiness may occur during nighttime, early-morning travel, or in dim vehicle environments. Facial measurements obtained through computer vision may be affected by lighting, camera placement, facial orientation, and image quality. DriveAlert uses an infrared-capable camera without dedicated infrared illumination. Its infrared capability does not itself provide light or guarantee usable facial measurements under extremely low-light or no-light conditions. DriveAlert therefore evaluates facial brightness and contrast before allowing calibration measurements or drowsiness-sign assessment. When lighting is insufficient, assessment pauses even if a face remains detected, and affected frames do not contribute to calibration’s required valid measurement time The system prompts the Driver to restore adequate visibility and resumes assessment when the applicable lighting-quality, recovery, and observation-freshness requirements are met. These checks are image-quality safeguards rather than measures of Driver drowsiness. Their suitability will be evaluated under different available-light conditions using the final hardware configuration.
[686] Drowsiness-stage warning intervention begins only after a selected sign satisfies its applicable measurement threshold and temporal-confirmation condition. Separate operational guidance may be issued when facial visibility or lighting is inadequate or when the movement-advisory condition is satisfied. Such guidance does not itself constitute a confirmed drowsiness-sign event, create an additional alert-history episode, or advance the Warning Stage. Detection of a qualifying visible behavior and selection of the corresponding intervention are therefore treated as separate processes. Ayas et al. (2024), in a scoping review of Driver-state-monitoring intervention studies, found that multistage interventions were commonly used and that auditory warnings were among the most frequently employed warning modalities. The reviewed systems generally progressed from an initial caution toward stronger warnings, requested Driver actions, or additional interventions. These findings support progressive warning intervention rather than requiring every confirmed event to produce an identical response.
[687] Schwarz et al. (2015) provide a direct basis for DriveAlert’s warning-stage transition architecture. Their drowsiness-mitigation system used a three-stage audio-visual warning in which the same drowsiness threshold was used to trigger each stage. Detection of drowsiness while no mitigation was active initiated Stage 1. During Stage 1, the detection algorithm could either continue classifying the Driver as drowsy or return to an awake state. If the Driver returned to an awake classification, the mitigation was allowed to abate after a fixed delay. However, if another drowsy episode was detected before the current mitigation abated, or if the drowsy state persisted for 60 seconds, the system escalated to Stage 2. Schwarz et al. applied the same logic during Stage 2, allowing the mitigation to either abate to Stage 1 or escalate to Stage 3. Stage 3 represented the maximum intervention level; further escalation was not permitted, although the Stage 3 warning could be reissued when drowsiness persisted or recurred soon afterward. The system permitted only incremental escalation and abatement between adjacent stages. 
[688] Gaspar et al. (2017) later experimentally evaluated a related staged drowsiness-countermeasure architecture using 72 participants in a high-fidelity driving simulator. Their Driver-state algorithm issued awake or drowsy assessments every six seconds, and the staged interface consisted of three increasingly urgent warning levels. In the evaluated implementation, each countermeasure state expired after 60 seconds. If the Driver was classified as awake when the state expired, the system returned to the previous stage; if drowsiness continued, the system progressed to the next stage. When already at Stage 3, continued drowsiness caused the Stage 3 warning to be triggered again rather than creating an additional stage. Gaspar et al. found that the multistage condition reduced drowsy lane departures relative to the single-stage condition, particularly during the early-morning drives in which participants were expected to be drowsier. These findings provide experimental support for progressive multistage intervention while Schwarz et al. (2015) provides the more detailed basis for the transition mechanics adopted by DriveAlert. 
[689] DriveAlert will adapt the three-stage transition principles described by Schwarz et al. (2015) to its discrete computer-vision warning events. Because DriveAlert does not repeatedly classify the Driver simply as awake or drowsy, a new distinct confirmed warning event will represent a newly occurring qualifying episode for warning-stage progression. A distinct confirmed warning event is created only after prolonged eye closure, yawning, or head nodding satisfies its complete Driver-specific measurement and temporal-confirmation condition. A single continuous qualifying behavior does not repeatedly generate new events, and qualifying signs occurring during the same overlapping behavioral episode may remain associated with one alert record. These event-management rules prevent a continuous or overlapping episode from causing artificial stage escalation.
[690] The first distinct confirmed warning event occurring while no Warning Stage is active will immediately activate Warning Stage 1 and begin a 60-second Stage 1 evaluation period. If another distinct confirmed warning event occurs before the Stage 1 period ends, DriveAlert will interpret the new event as recurrence of qualifying warning activity and will immediately progress to Warning Stage 2. Progression to Stage 2 begins a new 60-second Stage 2 evaluation period. The system therefore does not require the complete Stage 1 period to expire when a new distinct qualifying episode has already occurred. This adapts Schwarz et al.’s (2015) rule in which another drowsy episode detected before the current mitigation abated could cause progression to the next stage. 
[691] If no additional distinct confirmed warning event occurs during the Stage 1 evaluation period, the system will evaluate the state of the qualifying behavior when the 60-second boundary is reached. If qualifying warning activity remains continuously active at the boundary, DriveAlert will progress to Warning Stage 2 and begin a new 60-second Stage 2 period. If the previously confirmed behavior has ended, no new distinct warning event has occurred, and no qualifying behavior remains active, the system will return to the normal state with no active Warning Stage. This persistence pathway adapts Schwarz et al.’s use of 60 seconds of persistent drowsiness as an alternative reason for escalation while retaining Gaspar et al.’s use of 60-second countermeasure-state boundaries for reevaluation. 
[692] Warning Stage 2 will follow the same transition principle. If another distinct confirmed warning event occurs while Stage 2 is active, DriveAlert will immediately progress to Warning Stage 3 and begin a new 60-second Stage 3 evaluation period. The new event must represent a genuinely distinct qualifying episode rather than the continuation of the event that previously caused Stage 2. If no new distinct warning event occurs during Stage 2 but qualifying warning activity remains continuously active when the 60-second Stage 2 period expires, the system will likewise progress to Stage 3. If no additional distinct event occurs and the qualifying behavior has ended by the Stage 2 boundary, the system will de-escalate to Warning Stage 1 and begin a new 60-second Stage 1 evaluation period. This preserves incremental progression and de-escalation between adjacent intervention stages.
[693] Warning Stage 3 is the maximum DriveAlert intervention stage. Additional distinct confirmed warning events occurring while Stage 3 is active cannot produce a Stage 4. Instead, a new distinct event may reactivate the Stage 3 Driver-directed warning and will be recorded as recurrent qualifying warning activity during the current Stage 3 period. The 60-second Stage 3 evaluation period will not be converted into an additional intervention level. When the Stage 3 period expires, DriveAlert will evaluate whether recurrent qualifying warning activity occurred during the completed period or whether a qualifying behavior remains persistently active at the boundary. If either condition is present, DriveAlert will remain at Stage 3, begin another 60-second Stage 3 evaluation period, and may reissue the Stage 3 intervention. If neither recurrent nor persistent qualifying warning activity is present, the system will de-escalate to Warning Stage 2. Continued absence of qualifying warning activity subsequently permits incremental de-escalation from Stage 2 to Stage 1 and from Stage 1 to the normal state. This maximum-stage behavior follows the principle described by Schwarz et al. (2015), in which Stage 3 could not escalate further but its warning could be reissued when drowsiness persisted or soon recurred. 
[694] The 60-second value is not used as a confirmation threshold for prolonged eye closure, yawning, or head nodding. Those visible signs retain their independent Driver-specific measurement and temporal-confirmation conditions. Instead, 60 seconds represents the warning-stage evaluation interval used to determine whether persistent warning activity requires escalation or whether the absence of renewed qualifying activity permits de-escalation. A new distinct confirmed warning event may therefore cause earlier progression from Stage 1 to Stage 2 or from Stage 2 to Stage 3 without waiting for the corresponding 60-second period to expire. Conversely, when no new distinct event occurs, the 60-second boundary provides a defined point for evaluating persistent activity or recovery. Schwarz et al. (2015) directly described progression when another drowsy episode occurred before mitigation abated or when the drowsy state persisted for 60 seconds, while Gaspar et al. (2017) experimentally evaluated three-stage warnings using 60-second countermeasure states. 
[695] Because Schwarz et al. (2015) and Gaspar et al. (2017) used a Driver-state algorithm that classified the Driver as awake or drowsy rather than DriveAlert’s separate EAR-, MAR-, and head-pose-based events, DriveAlert’s mapping of a new distinct confirmed warning event to a newly occurring qualifying episode is an adaptation rather than a direct replication of their detection algorithm. Similarly, DriveAlert interprets continued satisfaction of a selected sign’s confirmed condition at a stage boundary as evidence of persistent qualifying warning activity rather than claiming that the Driver has been continuously and medically confirmed as drowsy. The suitability of this mapping and the 60-second evaluation interval will therefore be examined during controlled testing using the actual DriveAlert implementation.
[696] For example, if a Driver first produces a confirmed prolonged-eye-closure event while no stage is active, DriveAlert immediately activates Stage 1. If the eye closure ends and the Driver later produces a distinct confirmed yawning event while Stage 1 remains active, the new event immediately progresses the system to Stage 2 and begins a new Stage 2 evaluation period. If a subsequent distinct head-nodding event occurs while Stage 2 is active, the system immediately progresses to Stage 3. In contrast, when additional signs become confirmed while an existing alert episode remains active under the episode-continuity rule, their signs are merged into that episode and do not independently advance the Warning Stage. Likewise, during uninterrupted valid observation, keeping the eyes continuously closed after one prolonged-eye-closure confirmation does not repeatedly create new distinct alert episodes. Current-stage warning reminders may still be requested, while continued qualifying activity is separately evaluated at the applicable warning-stage boundary.
[697] Warning Stage 1 will activate the Driver’s selected predefined warning sound through the IoT device. This represents the initial Driver-directed intervention following a confirmed warning event. Auditory warnings are widely represented in the Driver-state-monitoring literature reviewed by Ayas et al. (2024), while Chandana and Sangeetha (2023) similarly used a sound warning at the first level of their three-level system.
[698] Warning Stage 2 will retain the Driver’s selected predefined warning sound without adding a spoken advisory. It serves as an intermediate warning state when qualifying warning activity recurs during Stage 1 or remains persistently active at the Stage 1 evaluation boundary. Although Stages 1 and 2 use the same audio output, they represent different positions within the warning-stage transition process. Ayas et al. (2024) support the broader use of staged interventions, while Chandana and Sangeetha (2023) provide precedent for introducing human-voice warnings after initial sound alerts. DriveAlert adapts these principles by reserving the spoken rest advisory for Warning Stage 3, where it is intended to distinguish the maximum intervention stage from the preceding sound-only warnings. The placement of speech at Stage 3 is a DriveAlert design decision rather than an arrangement directly prescribed by these sources.
[699] Chandana and Sangeetha (2023) provide a more direct precedent for reserving external communication for a final intervention level. Their three-level system initially provided a sound warning, progressed to a human-voice warning following repeated warning activity, and used its third level as an alert-notification stage in which information including GPS data was transmitted to the vehicle owner or another concerned person. Their implementation therefore demonstrates an intervention structure progressing from an initial Driver-directed warning, to a stronger Driver-directed warning, and finally to external awareness.
[700] Multi-feature Driver-monitoring approaches can reduce dependence on a single facial indicator when visual information is partially obstructed. Jia et al. (2021, 2022) demonstrated that monitoring could continue using the remaining observable facial features when the eyes or mouth were affected by sunglasses or face masks. DriveAlert applies this principle to supported mask and sunglasses detection and to facial measurements reported as unavailable by its processing pipeline. It does not claim to recognize every possible obstruction, such as all hands, phones, hair, scarves, or other objects. When one of the three monitored signs becomes unavailable while two remain reliable, DriveAlert continues in a degraded monitoring state and informs the Driver which capability is unavailable. If any two signs become unavailable, DriveAlert pauses drowsiness-sign monitoring rather than relying on a single behavioral indicator and instructs the Driver to restore sufficient facial visibility before monitoring resumes.
[701] Awasekar et al. (2018) provide additional precedent for placing external communication at a highest warning stage. Their Driver Fatigue Detection and Alert System used three fatigue stages based on its own eye-closure and yawning conditions. At Stage 3, the system combined its highest local intervention with communication to predefined contacts through an Android smartphone. DriveAlert does not adopt the fatigue-stage classifications used by Awasekar et al. because DriveAlert’s Warning Stages represent levels of system intervention rather than physiological or medical levels of Driver drowsiness. Their work nevertheless provides additional precedent for associating external communication with a final or highest intervention stage.
[702] Fitzharris et al. (2017) further demonstrated the value of supplementing Driver-directed warnings with external awareness in an operational Driver-monitoring environment. Their study examined a camera-based Driver-monitoring system that generated in-cab warnings and was subsequently combined with external monitoring and management feedback when fatigue events were identified. The external feedback condition produced a larger reduction in fatigue-event occurrence than the in-cab-warning condition alone under the study conditions. Although DriveAlert’s Trusted Contact function differs from fleet-management intervention, the study supports the broader principle that external awareness can supplement immediate Driver-directed warnings when monitored warning activity persists.
[703] Warning Stage 3 will combine the Driver’s selected predefined warning sound with a fixed prerecorded spoken advisory recommending that the Driver stop in a safe place and take a rest. It will also introduce Trusted Contact synchronization eligibility. When DriveAlert progresses from Stage 2 to Stage 3, one Stage 3 transition record becomes eligible for synchronization with the Driver’s approved Trusted Contact, subject to internet availability. The introduction of speech following earlier sound warnings adapts the progression described by Chandana and Sangeetha (2023), although DriveAlert places speech at Stage 3 rather than at their second intervention level. The exact advisory wording and its placement at Stage 3 are DriveAlert design decisions. Stage 3 represents the maximum system intervention level following recurrent or persistent qualifying warning activity; it does not establish a medically severe level of Driver drowsiness.
[704] The three Warning Stages must therefore be interpreted as levels of system intervention rather than measurements of drowsiness severity. Warning Stage 1 represents the initial Driver-directed intervention following the first distinct confirmed warning event. Warning Stage 2 represents an intermediate warning state that retains the selected warning sound when another distinct qualifying episode occurs while Stage 1 remains active or when qualifying warning activity persists through the Stage 1 evaluation boundary. Warning Stage 3 represents the maximum intervention level when qualifying activity again recurs during Stage 2 or persists through the Stage 2 evaluation boundary. DriveAlert does not infer that a Driver at Stage 3 has reached a medically established severity of drowsiness or is physiologically drowsier simply because the highest Warning Stage has been reached.
[705] Continued qualifying warning activity while Stage 3 remains active also requires an external-awareness rule that does not generate a separate Trusted Contact synchronization record for every confirmed event. Schwarz et al. (2015) retained Stage 3 as the maximum mitigation stage and allowed the Stage 3 warning to be reissued when drowsiness persisted or recurred. Gaspar et al. (2017) similarly retained Stage 3 as the maximum countermeasure state and retriggered the Stage 3 intervention when continued drowsiness required another escalation. DriveAlert adapts this repeated maximum-stage principle to its Trusted Contact synchronization management. 
[706] When DriveAlert initially enters Stage 3, one Stage 3 transition record becomes eligible for Trusted Contact synchronization. Individual distinct confirmed warning events occurring during the subsequent 60-second Stage 3 evaluation period continue to be stored in the Driver’s alert history and may reactivate the Stage 3 Driver-directed warning, but they do not each independently create a Trusted Contact synchronization record. Instead, they indicate that recurrent qualifying warning activity occurred during the active Stage 3 period.
[707] When the 60-second Stage 3 period expires, DriveAlert evaluates both recurrent and persistent qualifying activity. If at least one additional distinct confirmed warning event occurred during the completed Stage 3 period, or if a qualifying behavior remains persistently active at the Stage 3 boundary, DriveAlert remains at Stage 3, begins another 60-second Stage 3 evaluation period, and creates one Stage 3 persistence update that becomes eligible for Trusted Contact synchronization. If neither condition is present, DriveAlert de-escalates to Stage 2 and no Stage 3 persistence update is created for that completed period.
[708] If qualifying warning activity continues across several consecutive Stage 3 periods, at most one persistence update becomes eligible for synchronization for each completed Stage 3 period in which recurrent or persistent qualifying warning activity is established. Individual warning events remain available in the Driver’s detailed alert history but do not independently produce external synchronization records. This separates detailed event recording from the frequency of Trusted Contact updates.
[709] The exact rule of generating one Trusted Contact persistence update per completed qualifying Stage 3 period is not a notification frequency directly prescribed by Schwarz et al. (2015), Gaspar et al. (2017), Chandana and Sangeetha (2023), Fitzharris et al. (2017), or the other reviewed studies. Schwarz et al. directly support maintaining Stage 3 as the maximum intervention level and reissuing its warning when drowsiness persists or recurs, while Gaspar et al. directly support repeated Stage 3 intervention following continued drowsiness at a 60-second countermeasure boundary. Chandana and Sangeetha provide precedent for external communication at the final intervention level, and Fitzharris et al. support supplementing Driver-directed warnings with external monitoring or feedback. DriveAlert combines these principles by aligning Trusted Contact persistence updates with its Stage 3 evaluation boundaries. The resulting persistence-update rule is therefore a literature-informed DriveAlert information-management adaptation and will be evaluated during controlled testing for usefulness, unnecessary repetition, and synchronization behavior. 
[710] For example, several distinct confirmed warning events occurring during one Stage 3 evaluation period do not generate an equal number of Trusted Contact synchronization records. The events remain individually available in the Driver’s alert history, subject to the existing overlap and deduplication rules, while the completed Stage 3 period may produce one persistence update indicating that qualifying warning activity continued. This separates detailed event storage from external-awareness frequency.
[711] Trusted Contact synchronization remains subject to Driver approval and internet availability. Only eligible records from periods beginning on or after approval of the Driver–Trusted Contact relationship may be shared. Approval does not release the Driver’s earlier complete alert history, and continued access depends on the relationship remaining approved. If internet connectivity is unavailable when a Stage 3 transition or persistence update becomes synchronization-eligible, the corresponding record is retained in the synchronization queue until connectivity becomes available. Kashyap et al. (2025) demonstrated the use of Firebase and a web dashboard for remote review of Driver-status information, while Kumar et al. (2022) used SMS or email to provide drowsiness-related information to authorized persons or family members. These studies support the broader technical feasibility of making selected Driver information available to an authorized external person. DriveAlert limits this feature to secondary awareness and record review and does not operate as an emergency-response service, guarantee immediate assistance, determine whether the Driver is fit to continue driving, or permit the Trusted Contact to control the Driver’s monitoring session.
[712] The Driver’s drowsiness-alert history and basic Drowsiness Insights feature are supported by literature on recorded-event visualization and dashboard-based review. Murphy et al. (2021) and Moens et al. (2021) demonstrated that dashboards can support review of recorded safety-related events and trends. DriveAlert therefore allows Drivers to review confirmed warning events and basic descriptive summaries of stored records. These records and summaries are not intended as medical analysis, accident prediction, confirmation of drowsiness, or determination of the Driver’s fitness to continue driving.
[713] Overall, the reviewed literature supports the principal components of DriveAlert while showing that its three selected visible signs require different confirmation procedures. EAR, MAR, and head-pose measurements provide a basis for examining prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding, while Driver calibration addresses individual differences in facial measurements and neutral head orientation. Temporal evidence supports sustained confirmation of prolonged eye closure and yawning, while temporal persistence provides a stronger basis for interpreting ambiguous downward head movement. Driver-monitoring literature further supports progressive multistage warning intervention. Schwarz et al. (2015) provide the primary basis for DriveAlert’s three-stage transition mechanics by describing incremental escalation when another drowsy episode occurs before the current mitigation abates or when drowsiness persists for 60 seconds, together with incremental abatement and Stage 3 as the maximum intervention level. Gaspar et al. (2017) later experimentally evaluated a related three-stage architecture using 60-second countermeasure states and found that staged warnings reduced drowsy lane departures relative to single-stage warnings under the study conditions. Chandana and Sangeetha (2023) provide precedent for introducing human-voice warnings after initial sound alerts and external communication at a final intervention level. DriveAlert adapts these principles by retaining the selected warning sound during Stages 1 and 2 and introducing both a spoken rest advisory and Trusted Contact synchronization eligibility at Stage 3. Fitzharris et al. (2017) provide evidence that external awareness or feedback can supplement immediate Driver-directed warnings. DriveAlert adapts these principles to distinct confirmed computer-vision warning events and controlled Trusted Contact synchronization while treating its Warning Stages as levels of system intervention rather than medical levels of drowsiness. Mobile-processing and IoT literature further supports DriveAlert’s local Android processing and local wireless communication, while its performance under different lighting conditions will be evaluated using the final camera configuration.
[714] 
[715] Related Studies
[716] Deployed driver-drowsiness, fatigue, and alertness systems use mobile computer vision, wearable sensors, driver-condition assessment, and physical warning devices to help users recognize reduced alertness. Some systems monitor visible indicators such as eye closure and head movement, while others estimate fatigue using personal information, trip conditions, and reaction-time testing. These systems demonstrate that mobile, wearable, and embedded technologies can provide immediate warnings, although they differ in detection methods, hardware requirements, offline capability, record management, and user support.
[717] Drowsy Driving Alert is a deployed mobile application that uses artificial intelligence and the smartphone’s front camera to monitor the driver’s face and eyes. The application distinguishes normal blinking from extended eye closure and provides audio and visual alerts when drowsiness is detected. It can operate alongside Google Maps and Apple Maps, while facial processing is performed locally on the smartphone. However, the phone must be positioned where the camera can clearly view the driver. Lighting, camera obstruction, device performance, and background-operation restrictions may also delay or interrupt warnings.
[718] WingDriver is a smartphone-based driver-monitoring system that uses computer vision and artificial intelligence to detect drowsiness, fatigue, distraction, and inattention. It provides immediate audible or visual warnings to help the driver regain attention. The provider also offers related business solutions for fleets, ride-sharing services, insurers, and vehicle manufacturers. However, monitoring depends on the smartphone camera maintaining a usable view of the driver and on the mobile device having sufficient processing capability. Broader business deployment also requires coordination with the provider.
[719] Fatigue Alert – Stay Awake is a deployed application that uses supported AirPods or Beats headphones to monitor the user’s head posture. When downward head movement indicates possible drowsiness, the application provides warnings through sound, vibration, system notifications, or Apple Watch haptic feedback. It can continue operating in the background or while the device screen is locked. However, the system requires compatible Apple devices and supported headphones and focuses mainly on head-posture monitoring rather than facial indicators. It is also presented as a general stay-awake application rather than a system exclusively intended for driving.
[720] Anti Sleep Pilot is a dashboard-mounted device and mobile application that continuously calculates a driver’s fatigue level. Its calculation considers the driver’s personal risk profile, fatigue condition before the trip, and driving information registered through built-in sensors. It also administers periodic reaction-time tests and provides audible and visual recommendations when the driver should take a break. However, the system requires initial profile configuration and periodic interaction from the driver. Its documentation also notes that low-speed traffic, GPS conditions, and some driving situations may affect trip-state interpretation or cause distraction.
[721] The alertme Drowsy Driver Device is a commercially deployed wearable warning device developed by resqme, Inc. It is worn behind the ear and detects downward head nodding associated with possible dozing. When the user’s head tilts forward by approximately 15 to 20 degrees, the battery-powered device produces a 90-decibel audible alarm. The device does not require a smartphone, mobile application, or internet connection. However, it must be worn correctly, detects only head-nodding movement, and provides only a direct audible warning.
[722] The reviewed systems demonstrate different approaches to driver-state monitoring and warning. Drowsy Driving Alert and WingDriver use smartphone cameras and artificial intelligence to monitor visible driver behaviors. Fatigue Alert – Stay Awake uses wearable motion information to monitor head posture. Anti Sleep Pilot estimates fatigue through driver profiles, sensor information, and reaction-time testing. Meanwhile, alertme uses a simple wearable mechanism to detect downward head movement and produce an immediate alarm.
[723] The systems collectively show the progress and remaining integration gaps in driver-drowsiness and alertness monitoring. Smartphone-based applications provide accessible camera monitoring but depend on phone placement, facial visibility, and device performance. Wearable applications require compatible accessories, while fatigue-estimation systems require profile configuration and periodic driver interaction. Simple physical alarms can operate without internet access but generally monitor only one behavior and do not provide organized records.
[724] Overall, DriveAlert combines selected driver-monitoring and record-management functions in an external-camera and Android-based prototype. Its configuration includes an external IoT camera with an infrared-capable sensor, Android-based local processing, offline IoT-to-mobile communication, monitoring of prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding, and onboard speaker warning output. Confirmed warning events are stored locally for Driver review and descriptive summaries, while separate eligible Stage 3 transition records and persistence updates support approved Trusted Contact sharing. DriveAlert investigates whether these functions can operate together under controlled testing conditions.
[725] Comparative Matrix
[726] This section presents a comparative matrix developed to organize and assess relevant deployed systems in relation to DriveAlert: An IoT-Based Driver Drowsiness Monitoring and Alert System Using Computer Vision. It compares Drowsy Driving Alert, WingDriver, Fatigue Alert – Stay Awake, Anti Sleep Pilot, and the alertme Drowsy Driver Device according to their features, limitations, platform details, support mechanisms, and monetization schemes. The comparison identifies the different mobile, wearable, and embedded approaches used for driver-state monitoring and warning and supports the positioning of DriveAlert as an integrated driver-focused system.

[856] The comparative matrix presents five driver-drowsiness, fatigue, and alertness monitoring and warning systems. Drowsy Driving Alert and WingDriver use smartphone-based computer vision to monitor visible driver behavior and provide immediate alerts. Fatigue Alert – Stay Awake uses compatible wearable accessories to monitor head posture. Anti Sleep Pilot evaluates fatigue through driver information, trip conditions, sensor data, and reaction-time testing. The alertme device uses a simpler wearable mechanism to recognize downward head movement and activate a loud alarm.
[857] Clear distinctions exist among the systems. Drowsy Driving Alert focuses on facial and eye monitoring through the smartphone camera. WingDriver expands mobile monitoring to include drowsiness, fatigue, distraction, and business driver-safety applications. Fatigue Alert – Stay Awake depends on supported Apple accessories and monitors head posture. Anti Sleep Pilot does not primarily use computer vision but provides personalized fatigue calculation and break recommendations. The alertme device operates independently of mobile and internet connectivity but detects only head-nodding movement.
[858] DriveAlert combines external camera monitoring, Android-based detection, physical warning output, local record review, and controlled Trusted Contact sharing. It uses an external IoT camera with an infrared-capable sensor. The Android application receives the camera stream through a local Wi-Fi connection, evaluates prolonged eye closure, yawning, and downward head posture using the applicable measurement and temporal-confirmation conditions, and sends warning commands to the IoT device. Confirmed warning events are stored locally and summarized for Driver review. Separate eligible Stage 3 transition records and persistence updates are synchronized for access by an approved Trusted Contact when internet connectivity becomes available.

[919] Table 2 presents a comparative evaluation of DriveAlert alongside existing driver-drowsiness, fatigue, and alertness monitoring and warning systems: Drowsy Driving Alert, WingDriver, Fatigue Alert – Stay Awake, Anti Sleep Pilot, and the alertme Drowsy Driver Device. While each system addresses specific aspects of driver alertness, such as camera-based monitoring, head-posture detection, fatigue estimation, or audible warning, DriveAlert consolidates several of these functions into one driver-focused system. Specifically, DriveAlert combines an external infrared-supported IoT camera, Android-based local processing, offline IoT-to-mobile communication, monitoring of prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding, onboard speaker warning output, drowsiness-alert records, basic drowsiness insights, and Trusted Contact synchronization. Drowsy Driving Alert uses smartphone-based facial and eye monitoring and provides immediate alerts, but its publicly documented functions primarily focus on mobile detection and warning. WingDriver provides mobile computer-vision monitoring for drowsiness, fatigue, and distraction and supports driving-related information, but its documented individual-user platform relies mainly on the smartphone camera. Fatigue Alert – Stay Awake monitors head posture through compatible wearable accessories and provides sound, vibration, and smartwatch warnings, but it focuses primarily on head movement. Anti Sleep Pilot estimates fatigue through personal information, trip conditions, sensor data, and reaction-time testing rather than camera-based facial monitoring. The alertme Drowsy Driver Device provides a simple offline audible warning when downward head nodding is detected, but its documented monitoring function is limited to that movement. In contrast, DriveAlert integrates external camera monitoring, offline mobile processing, multiple visible signs associated with drowsiness, physical warning output, drowsiness-alert record review, and approved Trusted Contact access within one cohesive system.

[933] CHAPTER III
[934] RESEARCH METHODOLOGY
[935] This chapter outlines the software engineering methodology used in the development of “DriveAlert.” It includes the planning and initiation phase, program workflow, validation board, and Gantt chart. It also covers the analysis and design phase, including the use case diagram, user interface design, storyboard, database design, and network design for the connection between the IoT camera device, mobile application, drowsiness-sign detection process, alert mechanism, basic drowsiness insights, and Trusted Contact synchronization. The chapter further discusses the development and construction phase by presenting the technology stack diagram, software and hardware specifications, program specifications, and list of modules used in developing the system.
[936] Software Engineering Methodology
[937] The development process for “DriveAlert: An IoT-Based Driver Drowsiness Monitoring and Alert System Using Computer Vision” will be based on the Agile methodology due to its iterative and flexible approach, which supports continuous improvement and frequent testing of system features. In the case of DriveAlert, where the system involves driver drowsiness monitoring, alert triggering, drowsiness alert history recording, basic drowsiness insights, and Trusted Contact synchronization, Agile supports a development cycle that allows the researchers to improve the system based on testing results and user feedback. Agile’s emphasis on collaboration, feedback, and incremental progress provides a structured yet adaptable roadmap for the development process, helping the researchers manage system changes and ensure that the system requirements are addressed systematically.
[938] 
[939]

[942]

[950] Figure 2: Agile Methodology
[951] The team will use the iterative approach of Agile methodology to guide the development of DriveAlert and monitor the progress of activities throughout the project timeline. The project will be divided into ten two-week sprints, with each sprint following the Plan, Design, Develop, Test, Deploy, and Review activities. This approach is suitable because DriveAlert consists of several connected components, including the IoT device, mobile application, web portal, drowsiness-monitoring functions, warning system, drowsiness-alert record dashboard, and synchronization feature. Each sprint will produce a working system component that can be tested, reviewed, and improved before the next sprint begins.
[952] Planning Phase. The team will identify and organize the requirements for each sprint using the survey results, validation board, related studies, project objectives, scope, and approved system design. The team will define the expected outputs, required resources, task assignments, and testing activities. Early sprints will focus on research, documentation, requirements, and system planning, while later sprints will focus on development, integration, testing, deployment, and evaluation.
[953] Design Phase. The team will prepare and refine the designs needed for each system component. This phase includes the system architecture, diagrams, workflows, user interfaces, database design, network design, and testing procedures. The designs will show how the IoT device, mobile application, web portal, warning system, record-management functions, and synchronization feature will work together.
[954] Development Phase. The team will convert the approved designs into functional system components. Development will include setting up the required development environments, configuring the database and account services, assembling and programming the IoT device, developing the mobile application and web portal, implementing drowsiness-monitoring and warning functions, and creating the drowsiness-alert record and synchronization features. Each component will be developed gradually according to the assigned sprint.
[955] Testing Phase. Testing will be conducted during every sprint rather than only after the entire system has been completed. The team will test the functionality, accuracy, reliability, responsiveness, usability, and compatibility of each completed component. Testing will also cover device connection, video transmission, warning response, record storage, dashboard display, synchronization, low-light operation, and system performance. Identified errors, delays, incorrect detections, communication problems, and missing functions will be documented for correction.
[956] Deployment Phase. At the end of each development sprint, the completed component will be placed in the DriveAlert prototype environment for integration and verification. Early deployments will include the project foundation, database, account functions, IoT connection, and monitoring features. Later deployments will include the warning system, records, dashboard, synchronization, integrated application, web portal, and final IoT configuration.
[957] Review Phase. After each sprint, the team will review the completed work based on testing results, documented issues, and feedback. The review will determine whether the system requirements were achieved and whether revisions are needed. Identified improvements will be added to the project backlog and addressed in the succeeding sprint. The final review will verify that DriveAlert satisfies the study objectives, scope, system requirements, and evaluation criteria.

[1098] Planning-Initiation Phase
[1099] During this phase, the project's goals, scope, and requirements will be defined and clarified. This involves creating a Business Model Canvas to evaluate the business concept, a program workflow to illustrate user interactions with the DriveAlert application, and a validation board to identify the core problem and define a solution that addresses the needs of the intended users. Additionally, a Gantt chart will be used to outline tasks and their associated timeframes, and a functional decomposition diagram will break down complex processes into more minor, more manageable activities.

By diving into the Key Partners, Key Activities, Value Proposition, Customer Relationships, Customer Segments, Key Resources, Channels, Revenue Streams, and Cost Structure, the researchers will be able to gain comprehensive insights into the study's potential, uncovering the factors that aid to its success and areas that need improvement, making room for more informed decisions

[1160] Table 4 presents the Business Model Canvas for the DriveAlert system. The system serves drivers of four-wheeled vehicles and larger vehicles, and Trusted Contacts. Its key activities include developing the mobile and web applications, integrating the MediaPipe Face Landmarker model, calibrating drowsiness-sign detection thresholds, setting up and testing the IoT device, managing Alert records, and continuously improving the system. DriveAlert provides real-time drowsiness monitoring, offline local Wi-Fi operation, onboard speaker warnings, alert history and insight summaries, and Trusted Contact synchronization. Its key resources include the MediaPipe Face Landmarker model, cloud infrastructure, development tools, the DriveAlert IoT device, and synchronized alert records. The system’s possible revenue streams include the one-time purchase of the DriveAlert IoT device and a paid subscription for additional user of DriveAlert device.

[1180] Figure 3: Program Workflow (Drivers)
[1181] Figure 3 presents the program workflow for the Driver view of the DriveAlert mobile application. The process begins with account creation or login, followed by access to the Driver view and DriveAlert setup, which includes device connection, camera alignment, and Driver calibration. From the Home screen, the Driver may start monitoring, review Alert History and basic insights, manage Trusted Contacts, or access Settings. When starting monitoring, the system checks whether the required setup is complete. If further setup is needed, the Driver returns to the setup section; otherwise, the workflow continues through connector A, which leads to the separate monitoring workflow. In Alert History, the Driver may review recorded alerts and their details. The Trusted Contacts section allows the Driver to add a contact using a connection code and accept or decline incoming connection requests. Through Settings, the Driver may manage preferences, switch to the Trusted Contact view through connector B, or log out to end the workflow.

[1209] Figure 4: Program Workflow (Monitoring Session) 
[1210] Figure 4 presents the continuation of the Driver monitoring workflow from connector A. The Driver selects the facial visibility condition and starts a monitoring session. The system receives camera frames and checks whether the Driver can be assessed reliably. If assessment is unavailable, it displays connection or visibility guidance. Otherwise, it checks the available drowsiness signs and updates the warning stage. When required, the system activates or repeats the onboard speaker warning. Confirmed Alerts are saved or updated in local history, while separate Stage 3 sharing records are saved for synchronization with eligible approved Trusted Contacts when internet access is available. Monitoring continues until the Driver chooses to stop, after which the session is ended and saved. Connector C returns the Driver to the Home screen.

[1235] Figure 5: Program Workflow (Trusted Contact)
[1236] Figure 5 presents the program workflow for the Trusted Contact view of the DriveAlert mobile application. The user accesses this view after account creation and login or through connector B from the Driver workflow. The Trusted Contact may view Connected Drivers, manage Requests, or access Settings. Under Connected Drivers, the system checks whether shared records are available. If records exist, the Trusted Contact may review shared Stage 3 records and basic insights; otherwise, the system displays that no shared records are available. The Trusted Contact may also invite a Driver by entering the Driver’s connection code and sending a request. In Requests, incoming connection requests may be accepted or declined. Settings allows the user to manage account information, notifications, and history settings, switch to the Driver view through connector C, or log out to end the workflow.
[1237] Validation Board (Stages 1 and 2)
[1238] This section presents the validation board which is a strategic tool used in the development process to systematically test and validate assumptions about a product or service. It helps teams identify the riskiest assumptions, design experiments to test these assumptions, and gather data to inform decision-making.
[1239] Table 5.1
[1240] VALIDATION BOARD
[1241] Experiments
[1242] 1
[1243] 2
[1244] User
[1245] Drivers of four-wheeled vehicles and larger vehicles 
[1246] Potential Trusted Contacts of drivers
[1247] Problem
[1248] Drivers may experience drowsiness while driving and display observable signs such as heavy eyelids, frequent yawning, and head nodding. These signs may not always be recognized immediately, which may affect the driver’s attentiveness while operating a vehicle.
[1249] Trusted Contacts may need a practical way to remain informed about drowsiness-alert information shared by a Driver, particularly when records cannot be synchronized immediately because internet connectivity is unavailable.
[1250] Solution
[1251] Develop DriveAlert, an IoT-based driver drowsiness monitoring and alert system that monitors prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding, provides warning alerts, stores drowsiness-alert records, and 
[1252] Provide a Trusted Contact feature in DriveAlert that allows an approved Trusted Contact to review synchronized drowsiness-alert records and related information after the Driver has authorized access.
[1253] Table 5.2
[1254] VALIDATION BOARD cont’d
[1255] 
[1256] supports Trusted Contact synchronization.
[1257] 
[1258] Riskiest Assumptions
[1259] Drivers experience drowsiness or sleepiness while driving, find warning alerts helpful, and are willing to consider using an affordable and easy-to-use driver-monitoring system.
[1260] Potential Trusted Contacts are willing to review Driver-approved alert records, consider the shared information useful, accept delayed synchronization, and are willing to use an easy-to-use Trusted Contact feature.
[1261] Method & Success Criteria
[1262] A survey was conducted among drivers to assess drowsiness or sleepiness while driving, perceived helpfulness of warning alerts, and willingness to use an affordable and easy-to-use monitoring system. Success requires at least 70% positive responses for drowsiness experience and willingness to use the system.
[1263] A survey was conducted among potential Trusted Contacts to assess willingness to review shared records, perceived usefulness, acceptance of Driver-approved access and delayed synchronization, and willingness to use the feature. Success requires at least 70% positive acceptance of the Trusted Contact feature.
[1264] Result & Destination
[1265] The survey showed that 95.2% of 42 respondents had experienced drowsiness or sleepiness while driving, while 81.0% were willing to use a system like DriveAlert if affordable and easy to use. Since both exceeded 70%, development will proceed.
[1266] The survey showed that 88.2% of 17 respondents were willing to review shared records, 94.1% found the information useful, and 82.4% accepted delayed synchronization and would probably use the feature. Since the results exceeded 70%, the feature will proceed.
[1267] Learnings
[1268] The findings showed that drowsiness while driving is relevant among the surveyed drivers and that respondents showed interest in an affordable and easy-to-use monitoring system. These results support further development of DriveAlert’s monitoring and warning functions.
[1269] The findings showed that potential Trusted Contacts generally accepted Driver-approved access, delayed synchronization, and shared alert information. These results support retaining and refining DriveAlert’s Trusted Contact and record-sharing functions.
[1270] 
[1271] Experiment 1. Drivers may experience drowsiness or sleepiness while driving and display observable signs such as heavy eyelids, frequent yawning, and head nodding. DriveAlert is proposed as an IoT-based driver-support system that uses computer vision to monitor prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding, provide warning alerts, and store drowsiness-alert records. A survey was conducted among drivers to determine whether drowsiness or sleepiness occurs while driving and whether respondents are interested in an affordable and easy-to-use monitoring system. Success was defined as at least 70% of respondents reporting drowsiness or sleepiness while driving and at least 70% expressing willingness to use the proposed system. The results will guide the continued development and refinement of DriveAlert’s monitoring, warning, usability, and alert-record functions. 
[1272] Experiment 2. Potential Trusted Contacts may need a practical way to remain informed about drowsiness-alert information shared by an approved Driver. DriveAlert therefore includes a Trusted Contact feature that allows authorized users to review synchronized drowsiness-alert records and related information when internet connectivity becomes available. A survey was conducted among potential Trusted Contacts to assess their willingness to review shared records, perceived usefulness of the information, acceptance of Driver-approved access and delayed synchronization, and willingness to use the feature. Success was defined as at least 70% positive acceptance of the Trusted Contact feature. The results will guide the continued refinement of DriveAlert’s Trusted Contact, access-control, record-sharing, and synchronization functions.
[1273] Gantt Chart/PERT Chart
[1274] This section presents the researchers’ Gantt chart for the project. It will visually represent the project timeline, showing the start and finish dates of various tasks and activities. It consists of horizontal bars, with each bar representing a task, positioned along a timeline. Tasks are organized sequentially, with dependencies between them clearly marked, making it easy to see which tasks need to be completed before others can begin. The chart helps project managers track progress, allocate resources, and ensure timely completion of the project.

[4917] Functional Decomposition Diagram
[4918] This section presents the functional decomposition diagram, which illustrates the breakdown of various application processes into their respective components for a comprehensive understanding of the application 
[4919] 
[4920] 
[4921] 
[4922] 
[4923] 
[4924] 
[4925] Figure 6.1: Functional Decomposition Diagram
[4926] Figure 6.1 illustrates that the “DriveAlert” system is composed of several key modules: Account Management, Device Setup & Connection Management, Driver Drowsiness Monitoring Management, Alert & Warning Management, and Drowsiness-Alert Records & Trusted Contact Management. These modules work together to provide a driver-focused drowsiness monitoring and alert system. Account Management handles user registration, login, and profile information. Device Setup & Connection Management supports the required app permissions and the local offline Wi-Fi connection between the IoT device and the driver’s smartphone. Driver Drowsiness Monitoring Management handles the live camera feed and detection of selected visible signs associated with drowsiness, such as prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding. Alert & Warning Management manages threshold-based alerting, mobile notifications, warning-volume settings, and the activation of the onboard speaker using predefined warning sounds. The warning volume can be adjusted but cannot be muted or reduced below the enforced minimum level during active monitoring. Drowsiness-Alert Records & Trusted Contact Management stores drowsiness-alert history and supports the automatic synchronization of approved drowsiness-alert records to a Trusted Contact once an internet connection becomes available.
[4927] 
[4928] 
[4929] 
[4930] 
[4931] 
[4932] 
[4933] 
[4934] 
[4935] 
[4936] 
[4937] 
[4938] 
[4939] 
[4940] 
[4941] 
[4942] 
[4943] Figure 6.2: Functional Decomposition Diagram cont’d
[4944] Figure 6.2 presents two main modules of the “DriveAlert” system: Account Management and Device Setup & Connection Management. In the Account Management module, Drivers and Trusted Contacts can register, log in, view their profiles, update their account information, and log out of the application. This module allows each user to access the system based on their role, where Drivers can use monitoring-related features while Trusted Contacts can access synchronized drowsiness-alert records after approval. The Device Setup & Connection Management module enables the Driver to view setup requirements, allow the required app permissions, enable the mobile hotspot, connect the IoT device, view the device connection status, and reconnect the device when needed. This supports the proper setup of the IoT camera device and the driver’s smartphone before starting the drowsiness-monitoring process. Through these modules, DriveAlert ensures that users can securely access the application and that the device connection is prepared for real-time driver drowsiness monitoring.
[4945] 
[4946] 
[4947] 
[4948] 
[4949] 
[4950] 
[4951] 
[4952] 
[4953] 
[4954] 
[4955] 
[4956] 
[4957] 
[4958] 
[4959] 
[4960] Figure 6.3: Functional Decomposition Diagram cont’d
[4961] Figure 6.3 presents the remaining main modules of the “DriveAlert” system: Driver Drowsiness Monitoring Management, Alert & Warning Management, and Drowsiness-Alert Records & Trusted Contact Management. The Driver Drowsiness Monitoring Management module allows the Driver to calibrate the system, start and stop a monitoring session, preview the camera alignment, run drowsiness-sign detection, evaluate the selected visible signs associated with drowsiness, and view the current monitoring status. The system evaluates prolonged eye closure, yawning, and head nodding using Driver-specific thresholds together with the corresponding temporal or contextual confirmation conditions. The Alert & Warning Management module handles the warning process after a selected visible sign satisfies its complete confirmation condition. It allows the Driver to receive a confirmed warning event, determines the applicable Warning Stage, and provides the corresponding warning output through the onboard speaker. A threshold crossing alone does not immediately create a confirmed warning event. The Drowsiness-Alert Records & Trusted Contact Management module allows the Driver to view drowsiness-alert history, view alert details, view basic drowsiness insights, synchronize eligible alerts, and manage Trusted Contacts. The basic drowsiness insights include information derived from stored drowsiness-alert records, such as alert counts, detected-sign summaries, the highest Warning Stage reached, alert trends, and synchronization status. On the other hand, the Trusted Contact can view pending requests, accept or decline requests, view connected Drivers, and view synchronized drowsiness-alert records. Through these modules, DriveAlert supports Driver drowsiness monitoring, confirmed warning-event processing, Warning Stage management, warning output, drowsiness-alert record review, basic drowsiness insights, and synchronization of eligible drowsiness-alert records with approved Trusted Contacts when internet connectivity is available.
[4962] Use Case Diagram
[4963] This section presents the use case diagram, which provides a visual representation of the system’s functional requirements and user interactions. For “DriveAlert,” the diagram outlines the main scenarios where the Driver and Trusted Contact interact with the system to perform key functions related to account access, device setup, drowsiness monitoring, alert viewing, alert record management, and Trusted Contact sharing.

[5026] Figure 7.2: Use Case Diagram cont’d
[5027] Figures 7.1 and 7.2 presents the use case diagram of the “DriveAlert” system. The diagram shows two main actors: the Driver and the Trusted Contact. Both actors can register, log in, view their profile, update their profile, and log out of the application. The Driver can view setup requirements, allow the required app permissions, enable the mobile hotspot, connect the IoT device, view the device connection status, and reconnect the device when needed. The Driver can also start a monitoring session, preview camera alignment, run drowsiness-sign detection, detect selected visible signs associated with drowsiness, view monitoring status, and stop the monitoring session. For alert-related functions, the Driver can receive drowsiness alerts, receive onboard speaker warning output, view alert status, view drowsiness alert history, view alert details, and view basic drowsiness insights. The Driver can also add Trusted Contacts and send Trusted Contact requests. The Trusted Contact can view pending requests, accept or decline requests, view connected drivers, view shared drowsiness-alert records, view shared alert history, view shared alert details, and view shared basic drowsiness insights. These use cases show that the Driver controls the device setup, monitoring process, drowsiness-sign detection, alert viewing, drowsiness alert summary review, and Trusted Contact request process. On the other hand, the Trusted Contact is limited to accepting or declining requests and reviewing shared drowsiness-alert records and related basic drowsiness insights from connected drivers. Unlike the Driver, the Trusted Contact does not control monitoring, device connection, drowsiness-sign detection, or warning output.
